     │                                                                         │
     │ - CLI: alerts forecast --at "2026-10-09T18:00" prints, per oblast, its  │
     │   state (quiet / under alert, for how long) and the curve.              │
     │ - Static HTML site (alerts report → site/index.html, GitHub Pages, no   │
     │   server), four tabs:                                                   │
     │   a. Forecast map. Oblast map at the latest data time. Quiet oblasts    │
     │      are coloured by P(start within H); oblasts under alert are         │
     │      coloured by P(end within H), in a second colour scale. Horizon     │
     │      selector; clicking an oblast shows its full curve.                 │
     │   b. Replay over the test month, built so it can be read without        │
     │      explanation:                                                       │
     │      - Timeline chart (main view): oblast selector + horizon dropdown.  │
     │        The predicted probability is a line; actual alerts are red bands │
     │        beneath it on the same time axis. A good forecast rises before   │
     │        a band starts.                                                   │
     │      - Side-by-side maps: one time slider. Left map = forecast made at  │
     │        that moment; right map = what actually happened in the next H    │
     │        hours.                                                           │
     │   c. Scorecard: skill against baselines per horizon, calibration plots, │
     │      per-oblast table, feature importance.                              │
     │   d. Data: hour-of-day and day-of-week profiles, oblast co-occurrence,  │
     │      regime shifts (Dec 2025 switch to raion-level alerts, Sep 2026     │
     │      yellow/red levels), list of excluded near-permanent raions.        │
     │                                                                         │
     │ Data                                                                    │
     │                                                                         │
     │ ┌────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────┬─────────── │
     │ ───┐                                                                    │
     │ │    Role    │                   Source                    │ Window     │
     │ (UTC) │                                                                 │
     │ ├────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────── │
     │ ───┤                                                                    │
     │ │ Train      │ Vadimkin official_data_uk.csv (scraped      │ 2025-12-01 │
     │ → │                                                                     │
     │ │            │ @air_alert_ua declarations)                 │            │
     │ 2026-07-28  │                                                           │
     │ ├────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────── │
     │ ───┤                                                                    │
     │ │ Validation │ same                                        │ 2026-07-29 │
     │ → │                                                                     │
     │ │            │                                             │            │
     │ 2026-08-28  │                                                           │
     │ ├────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────── │
     │ ───┤                                                                    │
     │ │            │ alerts.in.ua                                │            │
     │ ~2026-09-10  │                                                          │
     │ │ Test       │ /v1/regions/{uid}/alerts/month_ago.json,    │ → fetch    │
     │ time │                                                                  │
     │ │            │ all oblasts, fetched once                   │            │
     │   │                                                                     │
     │ └────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────┴─────────── │
     │ ───┘                                                                    │
     │                                                                         │
     │ - Not used: volunteer/eTryvoga data (it doesn't match the declarations: │
     │   ~30% of starts have no official counterpart and there are alerts      │
     │   never declared in western oblasts), weather (evidence is anecdotal;   │
     │   leak-free forecasts are expensive to get).                            │
     │ - Official CSV: every row appears twice, so keep one row per unique     │
     │   alert. It has a gap from 29 Aug to 1 Sep and stops on 7 Sep; neither  │
     │   period is used.                                                       │
     │                                                                         │
     │ From raw alerts to an oblast timeline                                   │
     │                                                                         │
     │ 1. Canonical table: oblast, raion, hromada, started_at, finished_at,    │
     │    all UTC, from both sources through one adapter each.                 │
     │ 2. Drop near-permanent raions: raions under alert more than 60% of the  │
     │    time in the training window only are dropped (frozen list, saved to  │
     │    artifacts/, shown in the Data tab). This is what makes Dnipro,       │
     │    Kharkiv and Zaporizhzhia forecastable instead of reading "100%       │
     │    alert" because of Nikopol-type frontline raions.                     │
     │ 3. Merge per oblast: the union of all remaining oblast-, raion- and     │
     │    hromada-level intervals gives one timeline per oblast: "some part of │
     │    the oblast is under alert". Quiet gaps shorter than 2 min are        │
     │    bridged so overlapping raion alerts don't flicker.                   │
     │ 4. Units: 24 oblasts + Kyiv City as its own unit. Luhansk and Crimea    │
     │    (permanent alerts, not in the data) are excluded.                    │
     │ 5. Mapping: alerts.in.ua location IDs are mapped onto the same          │
     │    oblast/raion names, reusing the parsing in                           │
     │    ~/$code/PET/silent-sky/backend/services/alerts_in_ua.py.             │
     │                                                                         │
     │ Step 0: check the test source before any modelling                      │
     │                                                                         │
     │ Pull the month for every region, then confirm:                          │
     │ - it contains raion-level declarations, not only oblast-level ones;     │
     │ - the IDs map 1:1 onto the official names;                              │
     │ - how finished_at is recorded after 6 Sep, when the yellow/red system   │
     │   abolished the second all-clear signal;                                │
     │ - whether alert types or levels are present.                            │
     │                                                                         │
     │ If raion-level coverage is missing, the fallback is testing on the      │
     │ official August data and saying so in the README.                       │
     │                                                                         │
     │ Models                                                                  │
     │                                                                         │
     │ Two discrete-time hazard (survival) models built from the same feature  │
     │ builder. "Discrete-time hazard" means the model predicts, for each      │
     │ future time bin k, the probability that the event happens in bin k      │
     │ given it hasn't happened before. The cumulative probability is P(event  │
     │ ≤ H) = 1 − Π_{k≤H}(1 − h_k). That gives the whole curve from one model, │
     │ and the curve can never go down as H grows.                             │
     │                                                                         │
     │ ┌──────────────┬────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────── │
     │ ──┐                                                                     │
     │ │              │        Start model         │         End model         │
     │  │                                                                      │
     │ ├──────────────┼────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────── │
     │ ──┤                                                                     │
     │ │ Forecast     │ every hour, oblasts quiet  │ every 15 min, oblasts     │
     │  │                                                                      │
     │ │ times        │ at t                       │ under alert at t          │
     │  │                                                                      │
     │ ├──────────────┼────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────── │
     │ ──┤                                                                     │
     │ │ Bins         │ 24 × 1 h (1–24 h)          │ 24 × 15 min (15 min – 6   │
     │ h) │                                                                    │
     │ ├──────────────┼────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────── │
     │ ──┤                                                                     │
     │ │ Event        │ the oblast timeline goes   │ the oblast timeline goes  │
     │  │                                                                      │
     │ │              │ from quiet to alert        │ from alert to quiet       │
     │  │                                                                      │
     │ ├──────────────┼────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────── │
     │ ──┤                                                                     │
     │ │ Reported     │ 1, 3, 6, 12, 24 h          │ 15, 30, 60, 120 min       │
     │  │                                                                      │
     │ │ horizons     │                            │                           │
     │  │                                                                      │
     │ └──────────────┴────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────── │
     │ ──┘                                                                     │
     │                                                                         │
     │ - Training rows: each (oblast, forecast time t) expands into one row    │
     │   per bin k up to and including the bin where the event happens. Label  │
     │   1 in that bin, 0 before it. If the data ends before any event (the    │
     │   "censored" case), the row stops at the last fully observed bin. The   │
     │   bin index k is a feature.                                             │
     │ - Expected size: about 1–2M start rows and a similar number of end      │
     │   rows. Both models are LightGBM.                                       │
     │                                                                         │
     │ LightGBM setup                                                          │
     │                                                                         │
     │ - objective=binary, no class weights, so probabilities stay honest.     │
     │ - learning_rate=0.05, early stopping on validation log loss.            │
     │ - Small grid on validation only, about 8 configurations: num_leaves ∈   │
     │   {15, 31, 63}, min_data_in_leaf ∈ {200, 1000}, feature_fraction 0.8.   │
     │ - oblast as a categorical feature: one pooled model across all oblasts. │
     │ - Calibration: check reliability on validation. If needed, apply        │
     │   temperature scaling: a single scalar applied to the hazard logits,    │
     │   fitted on validation. It keeps the curve rising with H, unlike        │
     │   per-horizon isotonic regression.                                      │
     │ - Frozen after validation. No retraining; the test set runs through the │
     │   frozen model once.                                                    │
     │                                                                         │
     │ Baselines (each must be beaten)                                         │
     │                                                                         │
     │ - Start model:                                                          │
     │   - Climatology: per oblast, hour of week and bin k, the empirical      │
     │     hazard over the last 8 training weeks.                              │
     │   - Logistic regression on the same expanded rows and features.         │
     │ - End model:                                                            │
     │   - Kaplan–Meier per oblast, conditioned on how long the alert has      │
     │     already run. This is the empirical "alerts that have lasted 40 min  │
     │     so far end within 15 min X% of the time" curve, and a strong        │
     │     baseline.                                                           │
     │   - Logistic regression.                                                │
     │                                                                         │
     │ Features                                                                │
     │                                                                         │
     │ Every feature is computed by one function features(events, t), used     │
     │ identically for training, validation, test, the CLI and the UI. An      │
     │ alert is visible at t only if started_at ≤ t. Its end is visible only   │
     │ if finished_at ≤ t; otherwise it counts as still active at t.           │
     │                                                                         │
     │ - Calendar: hour of day and day of week in Kyiv time. No date or trend  │
     │   index.                                                                │
     │ - Own oblast:                                                           │
     │   - current state duration: minutes since the last end (start model) or │
     │     minutes elapsed in this alert (end model);                          │
     │   - decayed alert-start counts with half-lives 3 h, 24 h, 72 h;         │
     │   - number of starts in the last 24 h;                                  │
     │   - share of raions active now (end model).                             │
     │ - Neighbours (hand-written adjacency map of 25 units):                  │
     │   - number active now;                                                  │
     │   - minutes since the last neighbour start;                             │
     │   - neighbour starts in the last 30, 60 and 120 min;                    │
     │   - for the end model, whether neighbours are still active or already   │
     │     clear.                                                              │
     │ - National:                                                             │
     │   - number of oblasts active now;                                       │
     │   - starts nationwide in the last 60 min;                               │
     │   - hours since the last national wave (≥15 oblasts starting within 30  │
     │     min);                                                               │
     │   - binary current state of each of the other oblasts, so the model can │
     │     learn routes (Sumy → Poltava → Kyiv).                               │
     │ - Structure: oblast (categorical) and bin index k.                      │
     │ - Lookback: at most 72 h, so the first 72 h of the test month are used  │
     │   only as feature history and are not scored.                           │
     │                                                                         │
     │ Leakage controls                                                        │
     │                                                                         │
     │ 1. One feature code path. features(events, t) filters the events        │
     │    itself. Training and serving cannot diverge.                         │
     │ 2. Property test (tests/test_leakage.py): for ~200 random t, features   │
     │    computed on the full data equal features computed on data truncated  │
     │    at t, where alerts that start after t are removed and end times      │
     │    after t are blanked.                                                 │
     │ 3. No training label crosses a split boundary. A training example at    │
     │    time t is kept only if t + longest horizon ≤ train end; the same     │
     │    applies to validation. Splits are separated by at least 24 h.        │
     │ 4. Training-only artifacts. The dropped-raion list, climatology tables  │
     │    and adjacency map are fixed from training data or written by hand.   │
     │    Early stopping, the grid search and calibration use validation only. │
     │ 5. The test set is touched once. alerts evaluate --split test writes    │
     │    reports/test_metrics.json. Its timestamp and the frozen model hash   │
     │    are logged in the README; no tuning afterwards.                      │
     │ 6. Telegram (stretch only): messages are used by message.date ≤ t, and  │
     │    edited messages are dropped, because their text may contain          │
     │    information added after posting.                                     │
     │ 7. Source shift is reported, not hidden. Training comes from official   │
     │    declarations before 6 Sep; the test month comes from alerts.in.ua    │
     │    after the yellow/red switch. The report compares the calibration     │
     │    plots across that boundary.                                          │
     │                                                                         │
     │ Measuring accuracy                                                      │
     │                                                                         │
     │ The headline is probability quality: when the model says 30%, an alert  │
     │ comes about 30% of the time.                                            │
     │                                                                         │
     │ - Brier score at each reported horizon: mean of (p − outcome)².         │
     │ - Brier skill score: 1 − Brier_model / Brier_baseline against           │
     │   climatology (start model) or Kaplan–Meier (end model). This is the    │
     │   headline number: >0 means the model beats history alone.              │
     │ - Integrated Brier score over the whole 1–24 h (or 15 min – 6 h) curve, │
     │   as a single number per model.                                         │
     │ - Log loss at each horizon.                                             │
     │ - PR-AUC for the start model: alerts are rare in the west, so ROC-AUC   │
     │   would look flattering.                                                │
     │ - Calibration: reliability diagram (10 bins) plus expected calibration  │
     │   error per horizon.                                                    │
     │ - Uncertainty: 95% confidence intervals for the skill score from a      │
     │   day-block bootstrap. Shows whether beating the baseline is real or    │
     │   noise.                                                                │
     │ - Breakdown:                                                            │
     │   - three oblast groups: west, centre/south, frontline. The headline    │
     │     excludes the frontline group, where even after raion trimming the   │
     │     results are near-trivial;                                           │
     │   - per-oblast table;                                                   │
     │   - by hour of day.                                                     │
     │                                                                         │
     │ Telegram ablation (stretch)                                             │
     │                                                                         │
     │ - Source: @kpszsu only, pulled through Telethon using the local         │
     │   telegram skill session (read-only, no channel joins), adapting        │
     │   ~/$code/PET/ballistic-kyiv/backend/scripts/pull_corpus.py.            │
     │ - Features: national counts in the last 30/60/120 min of posts matching │
     │   takeoff/launch patterns (MiG-31K, Tu-95/160, ballistic, launch),      │
     │   reusing the regex approach in ballistic-kyiv/backend/classifier.py.   │
     │   No geocoding.                                                         │
     │ - Reported as: the start-model skill score with vs. without these       │
     │   features, test set, same frozen protocol.                             │
     │                                                                         │
     │ Repo                                                                    │
     │                                                                         │
     │ kse/                                                                    │
     │   pyproject.toml          uv, Python 3.12: pandas, numpy, pyarrow,      │
     │ lightgbm, scikit-learn, httpx, plotly, typer, pytest                    │
     │   src/alerts/                                                           │
     │     sources.py            official CSV + alerts.in.ua -> canonical      │
     │ table                                                                   │
     │     timeline.py           raion trimming, per-oblast union, forecast    │
     │ times, hazard row expansion                                             │
     │     adjacency.py          oblast neighbour map                          │
     │     features.py           features(events, t)                           │
     │     models.py             climatology, Kaplan-Meier, logistic           │
     │ regression, LightGBM, temperature scaling                               │
     │     evaluate.py           metrics, bootstrap, reliability               │
     │     site.py               static HTML (Plotly + oblast GeoJSON)         │
     │     cli.py                fetch | train | evaluate | forecast | report  │
     │   tests/test_leakage.py                                                 │
     │   reports/                generated metrics + plots                     │
     │   site/                   generated HTML for GitHub Pages               │
     │   data/, artifacts/       gitignored, rebuilt by `fetch` / `train`      │
     │                                                                         │
     │ - Secrets: the alerts.in.ua token is read from the environment variable │
     │   ALERTS_IN_UA_TOKEN, never committed.                                  │
     │ - Oblast boundaries: OCHA admin1 GeoJSON, already in silent-sky's       │
     │   backend/data/.cache.                                                  │
     │ - Code style: no code comments, per Bohdan's conventions.               │
     │                                                                         │
     │ Submission                                                              │
     │                                                                         │
     │ - GitHub repo with a README covering:                                   │
     │   - the question answered;                                              │
     │   - data provenance (and why the volunteer data was rejected);          │
     │   - the leakage protocol;                                               │
     │   - results with confidence intervals;                                  │
     │   - the site link;                                                      │
     │   - known limits.                                                       │
     │ - AI log: full export of this Claude Code session.                      │
     │ - Reflection: ~100 words, plain text. What went wrong: the first        │
     │   proposal was built on the volunteer data, the cross-check showed it   │
     │   doesn't match the declarations, and the official CSV turned out to be │
     │   duplicated and stalled. How the approach changed. Why the final       │
     │   version beats the first attempt.                                      │
     │                                                                         │
     │ Verification                                                            │
     │                                                                         │
     │ - uv run pytest: the leakage property test passes.                      │
     │ - uv run alerts fetch && uv run alerts train && uv run alerts evaluate  │
     │   --split val: both models beat their baselines on validation before    │
     │   the test set is touched.                                              │

     │   the replay against known events (e.g. the 13–14 Sep mass strike).     │

     │   end semantics). Handled by Step 0, with the August fallback.          │
     │ - The yellow/red regime may hurt calibration on test. It is measured    │
     │   and reported, and it is a finding in itself.                          │
     │ - Few events in the west give wide confidence intervals. They are       │
     │   reported per group, never hidden in a pooled number.                  │
     ╰─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────╯

✻ Brewed for 39s · done Saturday 6:31 PM

❯ Explain again why do we drop out near permanent oblasts                       

⏺ We don't drop oblasts. Every oblast stays in the model and on the map. What we
  drop is a few raions inside an oblast, before merging them into the oblast's
  timeline.

  The problem:
  - An oblast's timeline is "some part of the oblast is under alert", the union
    of all its raions' alerts.
  - If even one raion is under alert almost constantly, the whole oblast reads
    as "under alert" almost constantly.
  - In the official data for June–August 2026, Dnipropetrovsk oblast had 1 quiet
    hour out of ~2,100, Donetsk 21, Kharkiv 156 and Sumy 235.
  - Both forecasts then become meaningless:
    - Start model: "will an alert start?" never applies, because the oblast is
      never quiet.
    - End model: "when will it be quiet?" always answers "never". That's correct
      for the merged definition, but useless.
  - Meanwhile Dnipro city is often quiet. The constant alert most likely comes
    from raions right at the front. I'm assuming Nikopolskyi, which sits across
    the river from occupied Enerhodar; I haven't checked yet which raions it
    actually is.

  The fix:
  - Remove raions that were under alert more than ~60% of the time in the 
    training period.
  - Merge the remaining ones.
  - For Dnipro, the forecast then describes the rest of the oblast, which does
    go quiet and alert again. Both models then have something real to predict
    there.
  - The list is fixed from training data only, so it doesn't leak, and it's
    shown in the UI ("forecast excludes: X, Y raions").

  What it costs:
  1. Some areas lose their forecast: people in a dropped raion get no forecast.
     For them the honest answer is "always under alert", and the UI shows
     exactly that.
  2. The threshold is a judgement call: 60% is arbitrary. I'd check on training
     data how sharply raions separate. If there's a clear gap (some raions at
     90%+, the rest under 30%), any threshold in between gives the same list.
  3. The list doesn't update: a raion that becomes near-permanent later, for
     example in the test month, isn't removed. We can report that.

  The alternative is the plain merge of all raions. It's simpler, but Dnipro,
  Donetsk, Kharkiv, Sumy and Zaporizhzhia then have essentially no usable
  forecast, and we report them as "always under alert".

  Before deciding, I'd like to check which raions are actually near-permanent
  and how clear the gap is. It's a quick read-only check on the official data.
  Should I run it?

✻ Worked for 12s · done Saturday 6:32 PM

❯ Yes check please and plan again after you check                               

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I ran the check, and it changes the plan: the 60% cut would have done more
  harm than good. Now only one raion is dropped, at a 95% threshold.

  Per raion, Dec 2025 – Jul 2026 (official data, 118 raions):
  - 63 raions are under alert less than 10% of the time.
  - 24 raions are above 50%, and they're spread evenly from 50% to 100%. There's
    no natural gap, so any threshold in that range is arbitrary.
  - The top ones:

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────┐
  │                Raion                │ % of time under alert │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ Nikopolskyi (Dnipropetrovsk)        │ 99.6                  │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ Pokrovskyi (Donetsk)                │ 94.0                  │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ All 7 other Donetsk raions          │ 88.8 each (identical) │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ Synelnykivskyi (Dnipropetrovsk)     │ 85.8                  │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ All 5 Zaporizhzhia raions           │ 75.1 each (identical) │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ Kharkivskyi (includes Kharkiv city) │ 69.2                  │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ Sumskyi (includes Sumy city)        │ 66.3                  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────┘

  Why the 60% cut would have been wrong:
  - Donetsk and Zaporizhzhia would vanish. All of their raions have exactly the
    same coverage, which means each alert is declared for the whole oblast and
    recorded once per raion. A 60% cut removes every raion, so the oblast would
    look 100% quiet: fake data.
  - Kharkiv and Sumy would lose their cities. The cut removes the raions that
    contain Kharkiv and Sumy themselves, so the forecast would cover everything
    except the place most people live.
  - Only Dnipropetrovsk actually benefits, going from 0.1% to 54% quiet.

  What the plan now says: drop raions under alert ≥95% of the time in training.
  That's exactly one raion, Nikopolskyi:
  - Dnipropetrovsk goes from 0.1% to 12.2% quiet, in line with Kharkiv (12.9%).
  - It's the raion-level version of what the dataset author already does for
    Luhansk and Crimea: exclude what is permanently under alert.

  Resulting frontline oblasts (share of time quiet in training):

  ┌────────────────┬───────┐
  │     Oblast     │ Quiet │
  ├────────────────┼───────┤
  │ Donetsk        │ 6%    │
  ├────────────────┼───────┤
  │ Dnipropetrovsk │ 12%   │
  ├────────────────┼───────┤
  │ Kharkiv        │ 13%   │
  ├────────────────┼───────┤
  │ Sumy           │ 23%   │
  ├────────────────┼───────┤
  │ Zaporizhzhia   │ 25%   │
  ├────────────────┼───────┤
  │ Chernihiv      │ 34%   │
  └────────────────┴───────┘

  - Start model: few quiet hours to forecast in these six, so they're reported
    as their own group and kept out of the headline number.
  - End model: plenty of data here; this is where "when will it be quiet?"
    matters most, so it's reported in full.

  I updated the data and scoring sections in both PLAN.md and the plan file to
  match, and recorded the 60% rejection on the vault page. Everything else in
  the plan is unchanged.

✻ Baked for 49s · done Saturday 6:36 PM

❯ i see why. But it feels not honest. What are other options we have?           

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ You're right to push on this. Any rule that removes the raions which make an
  oblast look "always under alert" can read as tidying the data until the model
  looks good. Here are the honest options, with numbers from the check.

  What makes this hard: Dnipropetrovsk oblast as a whole is under alert 99.9% of
  the time, but Dniprovskyi raion, where Dnipro city is, only 23.2%.
  Nikopolskyi (99.6%) and Synelnykivskyi (85.8%) are in the same oblast. Merging
  them into one timeline mixes very different places. Every option is a
  different answer to "what does 'Dnipropetrovsk is under alert' mean?"

  Option A: plain merge of all raions, nothing dropped

  - Meaning: "somewhere in my oblast there is an alert."
  - Honesty: fully honest; nothing is removed.
  - Cost: Dnipropetrovsk and Donetsk are effectively always under alert, so
    their forecast is trivial: "will start: n/a, will end: almost never". The UI
    shows that as is. Kharkiv, Sumy, Zaporizhzhia and Chernihiv (13–34% quiet)
    still work fine.
  - Report: the frontline group is shown separately and plainly marked
    "near-permanent". That is itself a finding.

  Option B: each oblast's administrative-centre raion

  - Meaning: the forecast for each oblast is for the raion containing its main
    city: Dniprovskyi for Dnipro, Kharkivskyi, Sumskyi, Zaporizkyi, Odeskyi,
    Kyiv City, and so on.
  - Honesty: nothing is dropped by a threshold, it's one rule for every oblast,
    and it matches where most people live. The other raions still feed in as
    features.
  - Cost:
    - The forecast no longer covers the whole oblast, and the UI must label it
      ("Dnipro (Dniprovskyi raion)").
    - Kyiv oblast has no centre raion (its capital is Kyiv City, a separate
      unit), so it needs a stated choice.
    - Donetsk's centre is occupied; Kramatorskyi is the de-facto centre, and it
      carries the same oblast-wide alerts (88.8%).

  Option C: forecast every raion (118 units)

  - Meaning: "is my raion under alert?". Most precise, with no merging and no
    dropping.
  - Honesty: the most honest representation of the data.
  - Cost:
    - Five times more units, and many western raions have very few events.
    - Needs a raion adjacency map (buildable from the OCHA raion boundaries
      silent-sky already has).
    - The UI map becomes a raion map.
    - It reverses your earlier choice that raions don't make much sense. But the
      frontline problem only exists because merging loses raion detail.

  Option D: the ≥95% rule from before (drop Nikopolskyi only)

  Defensible, since it mirrors how the dataset already excludes Luhansk and
  Crimea, but it's still a judgement call, and that's what you're reacting to.

  My view

  - The most honest is A: keep the full merge and let the frontline oblasts show
    as near-permanent.
    - It's the simplest rule, it changes nothing in the pipeline, and the end
      model still says something useful for Kharkiv, Sumy, Zaporizhzhia and
      Chernihiv.
    - The fact that Dnipro city is quiet 77% of the time while its oblast is
      "always under alert" goes into the Data tab as a finding, not hidden by a
      rule.
  - If you want forecasts that are actually useful for Dnipro, B is the cleanest
    rule-based alternative.

  Which do you prefer?

⏺ User approved Claude's plan
  ⎿  Plan saved to: ~/.claude/plans/snoopy-skipping-cake.md · /plan to edit
     Air Raid Alert Hazard Forecast — Plan

     (Mirror of ~/$code/personal/kse/PLAN.md.)

     Context

     KSE Agentic AI School, Stage 2: a Python mini-project on time series
     analysis of Ukrainian air raid alerts. Evaluate on the latest period with
     zero leakage: nothing used for training or as input may come from after the
     forecast time. Graded mainly on process (AI log), then on the artifact.

     The June 2026 cohort solved the same task with national daily counts +
     ADF/STL + SARIMA/ETS/Prophet + Streamlit. This project instead answers the
     question a person actually has, per oblast, at any moment:

     - Oblast is quiet: what is the probability an alert starts within the next
       1–24 h?
     - Oblast is under alert: what is the probability it is over within the next
       15 min – 6 h?

     Product

     One forecast per oblast per moment, shown as a probability curve over
     horizons.

     - CLI: alerts forecast --at "2026-10-09T18:00" prints, per oblast, its
       state (quiet / under alert, for how long) and the curve.
     - Static HTML site (alerts report → site/index.html, GitHub Pages, no
       server), four tabs:
       a. Forecast map. Oblast map at the latest data time. Quiet oblasts are
          coloured by P(start within H); oblasts under alert are coloured by
          P(end within H), in a second colour scale. Horizon selector; clicking
          an oblast shows its full curve.
       b. Replay over the test month, built so it can be read without
          explanation:
          - Timeline chart (main view): oblast selector + horizon dropdown. The
            predicted probability is a line; actual alerts are red bands beneath
            it on the same time axis. A good forecast rises before a band
            starts.
          - Side-by-side maps: one time slider. Left map = forecast made at that
            moment; right map = what actually happened in the next H hours.
       c. Scorecard: skill against baselines per horizon, calibration plots,
          per-oblast table, feature importance.
       d. Data: hour-of-day and day-of-week profiles, oblast co-occurrence,
          regime shifts (Dec 2025 switch to raion-level alerts, Sep 2026
          yellow/red levels), per-raion coverage vs the merged oblast (finding:
          Dnipropetrovsk oblast is under alert 99.9% of the time because of
          Nikopolskyi (99.6%) and Synelnykivskyi (85.8%), while Dniprovskyi
          raion, where Dnipro city is, is under alert only 23.2%).

     Data

     ┌────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────┬──────────────┐
     │    Role    │                   Source                    │ Window (UTC) │
     ├────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
     │ Train      │ Vadimkin official_data_uk.csv (scraped      │ 2025-12-01 → │
     │            │ @air_alert_ua declarations)                 │  2026-07-28  │
     ├────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
     │ Validation │ same                                        │ 2026-07-29 → │
     │            │                                             │  2026-08-28  │
     ├────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
     │            │ alerts.in.ua                                │ ~2026-09-10  │
     │ Test       │ /v1/regions/{uid}/alerts/month_ago.json,    │ → fetch time │
     │            │ all oblasts, fetched once                   │              │
     └────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────┴──────────────┘

     - Not used: volunteer/eTryvoga data (it doesn't match the declarations:
       ~30% of starts have no official counterpart and there are alerts never
       declared in western oblasts), weather (evidence is anecdotal; leak-free
       forecasts are expensive to get).
     - Official CSV: every row appears twice, so keep one row per unique alert.
       It has a gap from 29 Aug to 1 Sep and stops on 7 Sep; neither period is
       used.

     From raw alerts to an oblast timeline

     1. Canonical table: oblast, raion, hromada, started_at, finished_at, all
        UTC, from both sources through one adapter each.
     2. No raion is dropped. A per-raion check (Dec 2025–Jul 2026) found no
        natural gap between 50% and 100% coverage: any cut is a judgement call
        that either wipes out whole oblasts (Donetsk and Zaporizhzhia raions
        carry identical oblast-wide declarations, 88.8% and 75.1%) or removes
        the raions containing Kharkiv and Sumy themselves. Near-permanent
        oblasts are shown as they are, not tidied away.
     3. Merge per oblast: the union of all oblast-, raion- and hromada-level
        intervals gives one timeline per oblast: "some part of the oblast is
        under alert". Quiet gaps shorter than 2 min are bridged so overlapping
        raion alerts don't flicker.
     4. Units: 24 oblasts + Kyiv City as its own unit. Luhansk and Crimea
        (permanent alerts, not in the data) are excluded.
     5. Mapping: alerts.in.ua location IDs are mapped onto the same oblast/raion
        names, reusing the parsing in
        ~/$code/PET/silent-sky/backend/services/alerts_in_ua.py.

     Step 0: check the test source before any modelling

     Pull the month for every region, then confirm:
     - it contains raion-level declarations, not only oblast-level ones;
     - the IDs map 1:1 onto the official names;
     - how finished_at is recorded after 6 Sep, when the yellow/red system
       abolished the second all-clear signal;
     - whether alert types or levels are present.

     If raion-level coverage is missing, the fallback is testing on the official
     August data and saying so in the README.

     Models

     Two discrete-time hazard (survival) models built from the same feature
     builder. "Discrete-time hazard" means the model predicts, for each future
     time bin k, the probability that the event happens in bin k given it hasn't
     happened before. The cumulative probability is P(event ≤ H) = 1 − 
     Π_{k≤H}(1 − h_k). That gives the whole curve from one model, and the curve
     can never go down as H grows.

     ┌──────────────┬────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────┐
     │              │        Start model         │         End model          │
     ├──────────────┼────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
     │ Forecast     │ every hour, oblasts quiet  │ every 15 min, oblasts      │
     │ times        │ at t                       │ under alert at t           │
     ├──────────────┼────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
     │ Bins         │ 24 × 1 h (1–24 h)          │ 24 × 15 min (15 min – 6 h) │
     ├──────────────┼────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
     │ Event        │ the oblast timeline goes   │ the oblast timeline goes   │
     │              │ from quiet to alert        │ from alert to quiet        │
     ├──────────────┼────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
     │ Reported     │ 1, 3, 6, 12, 24 h          │ 15, 30, 60, 120 min        │
     │ horizons     │                            │                            │
     └──────────────┴────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────┘

     - Training rows: each (oblast, forecast time t) expands into one row per
       bin k up to and including the bin where the event happens. Label 1 in
       that bin, 0 before it. If the data ends before any event (the "censored"
       case), the row stops at the last fully observed bin. The bin index k is a
       feature.
     - Expected size: about 1–2M start rows and a similar number of end rows.
       Both models are LightGBM.

     LightGBM setup

     - objective=binary, no class weights, so probabilities stay honest.
     - learning_rate=0.05, early stopping on validation log loss.
     - Small grid on validation only, about 8 configurations: num_leaves ∈ {15,
       31, 63}, min_data_in_leaf ∈ {200, 1000}, feature_fraction 0.8.
     - oblast as a categorical feature: one pooled model across all oblasts.
     - Calibration: check reliability on validation. If needed, apply
       temperature scaling: a single scalar applied to the hazard logits, fitted
       on validation. It keeps the curve rising with H, unlike per-horizon
       isotonic regression.
     - Frozen after validation. No retraining; the test set runs through the
       frozen model once.

     Baselines (each must be beaten)

     - Start model:
       - Climatology: per oblast, hour of week and bin k, the empirical hazard
         over the last 8 training weeks.
       - Logistic regression on the same expanded rows and features.
     - End model:
       - Kaplan–Meier per oblast, conditioned on how long the alert has already
         run. This is the empirical "alerts that have lasted 40 min so far end
         within 15 min X% of the time" curve, and a strong baseline.
       - Logistic regression.

     Features

     Every feature is computed by one function features(events, t), used
     identically for training, validation, test, the CLI and the UI. An alert is
     visible at t only if started_at ≤ t. Its end is visible only if
     finished_at ≤ t; otherwise it counts as still active at t.

     - Calendar: hour of day and day of week in Kyiv time. No date or trend
       index.
     - Own oblast:
       - current state duration: minutes since the last end (start model) or
         minutes elapsed in this alert (end model);
       - decayed alert-start counts with half-lives 3 h, 24 h, 72 h;
       - number of starts in the last 24 h;
       - share of raions active now (end model).
     - Neighbours (hand-written adjacency map of 25 units):
       - number active now;
       - minutes since the last neighbour start;
       - neighbour starts in the last 30, 60 and 120 min;
       - for the end model, whether neighbours are still active or already
         clear.
     - National:
       - number of oblasts active now;
       - starts nationwide in the last 60 min;
       - hours since the last national wave (≥15 oblasts starting within 30
         min);
       - binary current state of each of the other oblasts, so the model can
         learn routes (Sumy → Poltava → Kyiv).
     - Structure: oblast (categorical) and bin index k.
     - Lookback: at most 72 h, so the first 72 h of the test month are used only
       as feature history and are not scored.

     Leakage controls

     1. One feature code path. features(events, t) filters the events itself.
        Training and serving cannot diverge.
     2. Property test (tests/test_leakage.py): for ~200 random t, features
        computed on the full data equal features computed on data truncated at
        t, where alerts that start after t are removed and end times after t are
        blanked.
     3. No training label crosses a split boundary. A training example at time t
        is kept only if t + longest horizon ≤ train end; the same applies to
        validation. Splits are separated by at least 24 h.
     4. Training-only artifacts. Climatology tables and adjacency map are fixed
        from training data or written by hand. Early stopping, the grid search
        and calibration use validation only.
     5. The test set is touched once. alerts evaluate --split test writes
        reports/test_metrics.json. Its timestamp and the frozen model hash are
        logged in the README; no tuning afterwards.
     6. Telegram (stretch only): messages are used by message.date ≤ t, and
        edited messages are dropped, because their text may contain information
        added after posting.
     7. Source shift is reported, not hidden. Training comes from official
        declarations before 6 Sep; the test month comes from alerts.in.ua after
        the yellow/red switch. The report compares the calibration plots across
        that boundary.

     Measuring accuracy

     The headline is probability quality: when the model says 30%, an alert
     comes about 30% of the time.

     - Brier score at each reported horizon: mean of (p − outcome)².
     - Brier skill score: 1 − Brier_model / Brier_baseline against climatology
       (start model) or Kaplan–Meier (end model). This is the headline number:
       >0 means the model beats history alone.
     - Integrated Brier score over the whole 1–24 h (or 15 min – 6 h) curve, as
       a single number per model.
     - Log loss at each horizon.
     - PR-AUC for the start model: alerts are rare in the west, so ROC-AUC would
       look flattering.
     - Calibration: reliability diagram (10 bins) plus expected calibration
       error per horizon.
     - Uncertainty: 95% confidence intervals for the skill score from a
       day-block bootstrap. Shows whether beating the baseline is real or noise.
     - Breakdown:
       - three oblast groups: west, centre/south, frontline. In training the
         frontline oblasts are quiet this share of the time: Dnipropetrovsk
         0.1%, Donetsk 6%, Kharkiv 13%, Sumy 23%, Zaporizhzhia 25%, Chernihiv
         34%. The headline excludes the frontline group, where the start model
         has few quiet hours to score; the end model is reported in full there.
         Dnipropetrovsk and Donetsk are marked "near-permanent" in the UI and
         report, with their trivial forecasts shown as is;
       - per-oblast table;
       - by hour of day.

     Telegram ablation (stretch)

     - Source: @kpszsu only, pulled through Telethon using the local telegram
       skill session (read-only, no channel joins), adapting
       ~/$code/PET/ballistic-kyiv/backend/scripts/pull_corpus.py.
     - Features: national counts in the last 30/60/120 min of posts matching
       takeoff/launch patterns (MiG-31K, Tu-95/160, ballistic, launch), reusing
       the regex approach in ballistic-kyiv/backend/classifier.py. No geocoding.
     - Reported as: the start-model skill score with vs. without these features,
       test set, same frozen protocol.

     Repo

     kse/
       pyproject.toml          uv, Python 3.12: pandas, numpy, pyarrow, 
     lightgbm, scikit-learn, httpx, plotly, typer, pytest
       src/alerts/
         sources.py            official CSV + alerts.in.ua -> canonical table
         timeline.py           raion trimming, per-oblast union, forecast times,
     hazard row expansion
         adjacency.py          oblast neighbour map
         features.py           features(events, t)
         models.py             climatology, Kaplan-Meier, logistic regression, 
     LightGBM, temperature scaling
         evaluate.py           metrics, bootstrap, reliability
         site.py               static HTML (Plotly + oblast GeoJSON)
         cli.py                fetch | train | evaluate | forecast | report
       tests/test_leakage.py
       reports/                generated metrics + plots
       site/                   generated HTML for GitHub Pages
       data/, artifacts/       gitignored, rebuilt by `fetch` / `train`

     - Secrets: the alerts.in.ua token is read from the environment variable
       ALERTS_IN_UA_TOKEN, never committed.
     - Oblast boundaries: OCHA admin1 GeoJSON, already in silent-sky's
       backend/data/.cache.
     - Code style: no code comments, per Bohdan's conventions.

     Submission

     - GitHub repo with a README covering:
       - the question answered;
       - data provenance (and why the volunteer data was rejected);
       - the leakage protocol;
       - results with confidence intervals;
       - the site link;
       - known limits.
     - AI log: full export of this Claude Code session.
     - Reflection: ~100 words, plain text. What went wrong: the first proposal
       was built on the volunteer data, the cross-check showed it doesn't match
       the declarations, and the official CSV turned out to be duplicated and
       stalled. How the approach changed. Why the final version beats the first
       attempt.

     Verification

     - uv run pytest: the leakage property test passes.
     - uv run alerts fetch && uv run alerts train && uv run alerts evaluate 
       --split val: both models beat their baselines on validation before the
       test set is touched.
     - uv run alerts evaluate --split test, run once.
     - uv run alerts forecast --at <time in test month>: compare by hand with
       what the replay shows for the same time.
     - uv run alerts report: open site/index.html, check all four tabs and the
       replay against known events (e.g. the 13–14 Sep mass strike).

     Risks

     - alerts.in.ua differs from the official declarations (coverage, IDs, end
       semantics). Handled by Step 0, with the August fallback.
     - The yellow/red regime may hurt calibration on test. It is measured and
       reported, and it is a finding in itself.
     - Few events in the west give wide confidence intervals. They are reported
       per group, never hidden in a pooled number.

⏺ Pulling the month for every region from alerts.in.ua (about 15 minutes at
  their rate limit). The first region already showed something important,
  explained below.

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The Nikopol mystery is solved, and it's worth knowing for the plan.

  - Alert types: alerts.in.ua labels each alert air_raid or artillery_shelling,
    and since September also with a red/yellow level. Raion and hromada levels
    are included, and the history goes back to about 10 Sep.
  - Artillery appears only at hromada/city level (Nikopol, Marhanets). In the
    official CSV, the hromada-level rows are exactly those artillery hromadas.
    - Nikopolskyi raion's 99.6% was artillery. With raion-level rows only it
      drops to 33.1%.
    - So the official data mixes in artillery alerts, even though the task is
      about air raids.

  That suggests a clean rule with no threshold and no judgement call: build each
  oblast's timeline from oblast- and raion-level declarations only. Applied to
  the official data, that removes the artillery and keeps the air raids.
  Dnipropetrovsk then goes from 99.9% to 88.5% of time under alert;
  Synelnykivskyi (85.8%, air raid) remains.

  When the full fetch finishes, I'll check two things across all regions:
  whether artillery ever appears at raion level, and how much air-raid time at
  hromada/city level isn't already covered by a raion alert. If both are about
  zero, the rule removes artillery and loses nothing.

⏺ Background command "Fetch alerts.in.ua month history for all regions"
completed (exit code 0)
  Ran 3 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The full fetch is done, and it changes the plan in an important way.

  1. The artillery rule works, with a cost

  - Where artillery appears: across all 26 regions, artillery_shelling occurs
    only at hromada/city level (Nikopol, Marhanets, a few Kharkiv hromadas),
    never at raion or oblast level. Taking only oblast- and raion-level alerts
    gives 0% non-air-raid time.
  - The cost: some real air raid alerts are declared only at city level, so the
    rule loses them: Kirovohrad 19% of its air-raid time, Kharkiv 10%, Dnipro
    4%.
  - Better rule for the test data: in alerts.in.ua we can filter by alert_type 
    == air_raid directly, so we don't need the level rule there.

  2. The real problem: "alert" means something different since September

  Since September, 63% of air raid alerts are yellow. Here's how much of the
  time each region is under alert:

  ┌───────────┬───────────────────────┬───────────────────┬────────────────┐
  │  Region   │ Training (official,   │ Test month, red + │  Test month,   │
  │           │       Dec–Jul)        │       yellow      │    red only    │
  ├───────────┼───────────────────────┼───────────────────┼────────────────┤
  │ Kyiv City │ 5.9%                  │ 31.1%             │ 2.6%           │
  ├───────────┼───────────────────────┼───────────────────┼────────────────┤
  │ Kyiv      │ 17%                   │ 67.7%             │ 11.4%          │
  │ oblast    │                       │                   │                │
  ├───────────┼───────────────────────┼───────────────────┼────────────────┤
  │ Poltava   │ ~32%                  │ 54.0%             │ 9.4%           │
  ├───────────┼───────────────────────┼───────────────────┼────────────────┤
  │ Odesa     │ ~22%                  │ 39.6%             │ 6.4%           │
  └───────────┴───────────────────────┴───────────────────┴────────────────┘

  - Kyiv City went from about 6% of time under alert to 31%, mostly long yellow
    (drone-threat) alerts. Late September had 12–18 hours a day under alert.
  - Before September, an alert was one siren-backed signal. Now it's two levels,
    and neither matches the old one:
    - red + yellow is about 5× the old rate;
    - red only is about half of it.
  - The criteria are also recalibrated weekly by the General Staff.
  - Consequence: a model trained on December–August data and tested on
    September–October would be wrong mostly because the definition of an alert
    changed, not because the model is bad. The old plan's train/test split
    doesn't work.

  Options

  A. Train and test inside the new system only (alerts.in.ua, 10 Sep – today)
  - Split: 72 h of feature warm-up, then train 13–30 Sep, validate 1–4 Oct, test
    5 Oct onward. Refetching on 12 Oct would add two more test days.
  - Data: about a month in total, the test part about a week (~25 oblasts × ~170
    h).
    - The start model gets roughly 1M hazard rows, which is fine.
    - Confidence intervals will be wide, and we'll say so.
  - Pre-September data is used for two things:
    - the Data tab (the regime shift is a headline finding);
    - one ablation: "does adding old data with a regime flag help?". It directly
      measures whether the old history still carries over.

  B. Old data for training, test on "red only"
  This assumes red ≈ the old alert. The numbers above show it isn't (half the
  old rate), so it would be built on a guess.

  C. Keep the original split and just report the shift
  Honest, but the result would mostly measure the definition change, and
  calibration would be badly off.

  Recommendation: A, with two targets

  1. "Any air raid alert" (red or yellow): what people see in the app.
  2. "Red alert": missile, ballistic or massed attack. Rarer and more serious.

  Both use the same pipeline; only the event definition changes. That fits the
  new system naturally, and the regime-flag ablation is a good finding for the
  interview.

  What it changes in the plan:
  - the data and split sections;
  - level and alert_type added to the canonical table;
  - the artillery question disappears, since we filter by alert_type;
  - frontline oblasts stay as in option A from before, i.e. near-permanent ones
    shown as is.

  Do you agree with A? And do you want both targets or only "any air raid
  alert"? Then I'll rewrite the plan.

✻ Brewed for 1m 6s · done Saturday 6:58 PM

※ recap: We're building an air raid alert forecast for your KSE Stage 2 project and found that alerts since September (yellow/red levels) mean something different from the older training data. Next, you decide: train on the post-September data only, and forecast any alert, red only, or both? (disable recaps in /config)

❯ yes I just totally forgot. we must think about this. From the user            
  perspective I think we need to show both predictions for alerts (red/yellow). 
  Im not sure though what do we with the older data? we can't just drop it out  
  completely, it gives us much bigger perspective and better honesty            

⏺ You're right that we shouldn't drop it. Eight months of history carry things
  the new month can't: how alerts spread between oblasts, the daily rhythm, how
  waves cluster. And a two-month test on a stable definition is much stronger
  evidence than one noisy week. Here's how I'd use the older data without mixing
  up the definitions.

  Two forecasts for the user: "any alert" and "red alert"

  I'd show "any alert (yellow or red)" and "red alert", rather than yellow and
  red separately:
  - A yellow alert often escalates into red. A "yellow only" forecast would be
    confusing: does red count?
  - "Any" and "red" are nested (red is a subset of any). They read naturally:
    "68% chance of some alert in the next 6 h, 12% that it's red."

  Each has a start model and an end model, so four models from the same code,
  with the event definition as a parameter.

  Old data has three jobs

  1. Prove the method on a stable definition (evaluation E1)
  - Train Dec 2025–Jun 2026, validate July, test 1–28 Aug, all on old official
    data with one consistent definition.
  - About 25 oblasts × 670 hours of test data gives tight confidence intervals.
    It answers "does the method work at all?" separately from "does it work in
    the new system?".

  2. Training data for the "any alert" model
  - Old and new rows are pooled into one model, with a regime feature (old/new).
  - The old alert ≈ "any" is the closest match: one signal for any air threat.
  - The model learns spread patterns, timing and wave dynamics from eight months
    of data. The regime feature lets it adjust how often alerts occur after
    September.
  - Red/yellow input features (e.g. "neighbour is under red alert") are empty
    for old rows; LightGBM handles missing values natively.

  3. Measure whether old data helps (the honest test)

  On the new-system test, compare three versions of the "any" model:
  - (a) new data only;
  - (b) old + new with the regime feature;
  - (c) old data only, applied as is.

  If (b) beats (a), old data has been shown to help. If (c) fails badly, that
  shows the definition shift in numbers. Either result is honest and
  interesting.

  The "red" model trains on new data only, because old rows have no red label.
  Red alerts are rarer: Kyiv City had about 20 in the month, but across all
  oblasts there are enough for a pooled model. The "any" forecast probability is
  one of its features, so red benefits from the old data indirectly.

  Splits

  - E1, old system: train Dec–Jun, validate July, test 1–28 Aug.
  - E2, new system (the task's "latest period"):
    - 10–12 Sep: feature warm-up only;
    - train 13–27 Sep (plus old data for "any");
    - validate 28 Sep – 1 Oct;
    - test 2 Oct onward. A refetch on 12 Oct adds two more test days.
  - The alerts.in.ua history is a rolling 30-day window. Each fetch must be
    added to the saved data; replacing it would lose the start of September.

  Artillery: one rule for both systems

  - The old data has no alert type, so I'd use the level rule everywhere:
    oblast- and raion-level declarations only. In the new data that removes 100%
    of artillery alerts.
  - Using the same rule in the new data keeps "any alert" defined identically in
    both systems.
  - Cost: some city-only air raid alerts are lost in Kirovohrad (19% of its
    air-raid time), Kharkiv (10%) and Dnipro (4%). It's documented, not hidden.

  Does this match what you meant by perspective and honesty? If yes, I'll
  rewrite the plan around it.

          -nt happens. Label 1 in that bin, 0 before it. If the data ends
          - before any event (the "censored" case), the row stops at the 
          -last fully observed bin. The bin index `k` is a feature.      
       66 -- **Expected size:** about 1–2M start rows and a similar numbe
          -r of end rows. Both models are LightGBM.

      107

           honest.

           ture_fraction` 0.8.

          -ll oblasts.

          - apply temperature scaling: a single scalar applied to the haz
          -ard logits, fitted on validation. It keeps the curve rising wi
          -th H, unlike per-horizon isotonic regression.

          -s through the frozen model once.

      170  ## Measuring accuracy
      171  
      121 -The headline is probability quality: when the model says 30%, 
          -an alert comes about 30% of the time.                         
      172 +The headline is probability quality: when the model says 30%, 
          +the event happens about 30% of the time.                      
      173  
      174  - **Brier score** at each reported horizon: mean of (p − outco
           me)².
      124 -- **Brier skill score:** `1 − Brier_model / Brier_baseline` ag
          -ainst climatology (start model) or Kaplan–Meier (end model). T
          -his is **the headline number**: >0 means the model beats histo
          -ry alone.                                                     
      125 -- **Integrated Brier score** over the whole 1–24 h (or 15 min 
          -– 6 h) curve, as a single number per model.                   
      175 +- **Brier skill score:** `1 − Brier_model / Brier_baseline` ag
          +ainst climatology (start models) or Kaplan–Meier (end models).
          + This is **the headline number**: >0 means the model beats his
          +tory alone.

⏺ I rewrote the plan around the two alert levels and the old data. It's in
  ~/$code/personal/kse/PLAN.md, with the same copy in the plan file.

  What changed:
  - Two levels, four models: start and end models for "any alert (yellow or
    red)" and for "red alert", all from one parametrised pipeline. The UI and
    CLI show both, with an Any/Red toggle. Red is capped so it can never exceed
    Any.
  - How the old data is used:
    - "Any" models: trained on old and new data together, with a regime feature.
    - "Red" models: trained on new data only. They use the "any" forecast as an
      input, so they benefit from the old data indirectly.
    - Ablation: new data only vs. old + new vs. old only. This shows whether old
      data helps and puts the regime shift into numbers.
  - Two evaluations:
    - E1, old system: a method check on August, tested on about four weeks of
      data with a stable definition, so tight confidence intervals.
    - E2, new system: the latest period the task asks for, tested from 2 Oct
      onward. Confidence intervals will be wide, and we'll say so.
  - Artillery: one rule for both systems, using only oblast- and raion-level
    declarations. It removes all artillery alerts. The cost is losing some
    city-only air raid alerts in Kirovohrad, Kharkiv and Dnipro, which is
    documented.
  - The alerts.in.ua history is a rolling 30-day window, so the store is
    append-only. Today's raw fetch is saved in
    data/raw/alerts_in_ua_2026-10-10/; without it, the early September data
    would disappear from the API.
  - New "Key data findings" section at the top of the plan: the regime shift
    table, artillery, the volunteer-data rejection and the duplicated official
    CSV. This is the material for the README and the reflection.

  The vault has the regime-shift numbers, the artillery finding and the
  decisions.

  Shall I start building? I'd begin with sources.py and timeline.py and check
  that the before/after numbers come out of the real pipeline.

  per-oblast, per-hour patterns they haven't learned. I'll test boosting on top

  Read 2 files, ran 4 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I've built the whole pipeline. E1 is tested and E2 is trained and validated. I
  stopped here because four decisions are yours. I recorded everything in the
  Second Brain: the KSE page, the October ingest ledger, and a new KSE line in
  CRITICAL_FACTS.md.

  Results so far

  - E1 test (old system, August, run once and logged): the start model beats
    climatology by +10% at 1 h, +15% at 3 h, +12% at 6 h and +7% at 12 h. The
    end model beats the Kaplan–Meier baseline by +2.5% to +5.8%. All confidence
    intervals are above zero except the start model at 24 h.
  - E2 validation (new system, only 4 days): any-start gains +7% to +16%.
    - Old data helps: the model trained on old + new data beats both new-only
      and old-only. Old-only collapses to −19% at 24 h, which is the regime
      shift in numbers.
    - any-end gains only at 2 h (+4%).
    - The red models show no skill over climatology. There are only about 3,000
      red events to learn from.

  Things I changed along the way (worth mentioning in your reflection)

  - LightGBM lost to plain logistic regression on E1 starts. Trees learned on
    December–June didn't carry over to July. So each model now trains both, and
    the one with the better validation score is used. Logistic won the E1
    starts; LightGBM won everything in E2 except red-start.
  - Possible leak in the red level. alerts.in.ua's history keeps a single
    red/yellow level per alert. If a yellow alert is upgraded to red in place,
    its history row looks red from the start. I removed red-based inputs from
    the "any" models, which also slightly improved them. A poller is checking
    live alerts for this; nothing confirmed yet.
  - Two site bugs fixed: the Plotly CDN link returned 404, and the map shapes
    were filled inside-out.
  - Leakage test: it passes, and I checked that it fails when I inject a
    deliberate look-ahead feature.
  - Known weakness: the start model is overconfident above ~60%. On validation,
    forecasts of 65–72% came true about 50% of the time. It's documented in the
    README.

  The site is at site/index.html, with all four tabs checked in screenshots.

  Decisions for you

  1. E2 test timing. I'd run it once on Monday, 12 Oct, after re-fetching
     alerts.in.ua (adds two test days, about 15 minutes). By then the poller
     will have two days of evidence on the red upgrades. The poller runs in the
     background and stops if the laptop sleeps.
  2. The red forecast in the product. I'd keep it, labelled "no better than
     historical rates yet". The alternative is hiding it.
  3. Overconfidence. I'd report it rather than add another calibration step
     tuned on a 4-day validation window.
  4. GitHub. I haven't committed or pushed anything. Should I create a public
     repo, commit and push, and turn on GitHub Pages for the site? Raw data
     stays out of the repo; alerts fetch rebuilds it.
